# The Book /
# of METEOROLOGY, <note>original Latin: "METEORORVM"</note>
### of the Noble and Highly Learned Lord
### AVREOLUS THEOPHRASTUS
### of Hohenheim, called Paracelsus,
### Doctor of Medicine. <term>Doctor of both medicines (original: "beider Artzney"): a title indicating the author was a doctor of both internal medicine (physic) and surgery.</term>

### Also:

### THE FOURTH BOOK <note>original Latin: "LIBER QVARTVS"</note> OF THE
### Paramirum <note>The "Paramirum" is one of Paracelsus's foundational medical texts, exploring the five "Entia" or causes of disease.</note>
### concerning the Womb. <note>original Latin: "de Matrice"</note>

->Never before issued in print.<-

->With Imperial Grace and Privilege. <note>A legal protection granted by the Holy Roman Emperor to prevent unauthorized reprinting of the book for a set period.</note><-

->Printed at Cologne by the heirs of Arnold Birckmann.<-
->In the year 1566.<-

<summary>This is the title page for a 1566 publication featuring two distinct works by the Renaissance physician Paracelsus: a treatise on atmospheric phenomena and a medical text regarding women's health.</summary>

**Buried here is Philip**
**Theophrastus, distinguished**
**Doctor of Medicine, who**
**through wonderful art**
**removed those dire wounds,**
**leprosy, gout, dropsy,** <note>original Latin: "Lepram, Podagram, Hydropisim"</note>
**and other incurable**
**contagions of the body;**
**and he honored his**
**goods by distributing**
**and bestowing them**
**upon the poor. In the**
**year 1541, on the 24th**
**day of September, he**
**exchanged life for death.**

<page-num>1</page-num>
<header>BOOK OF METE-</header>

# ORS BY THEOPHRASTUS
### of Hohenheim, German Philosopher, concerning meteoric phenomena from the Element of Fire or the Heavens, and their impressions. <note>In the 16th century, "meteors" referred to any atmospheric or celestial phenomenon, including lightning, comets, and rainbows, not just falling stars.</note>

When God wished to create the earth, and in His Divine wisdom considered how and in what way it should be, He divided it into four parts, so that there are four <term>bodies</term> <note>original Latin: "Corpora". Paracelsus uses this to mean the fundamental physical manifestations of the elements.</note>, which bodies were to be mothers of all things belonging to the one He would create in His image—that is, Adam, which is Man. Now, once this purpose was decided by God, the four bodies were created: namely, Heaven, Earth, Water, and Air. For as the Scripture says, He first created the Heaven, then the Earth, followed by the others of like kind.

<sig>A</sig> <meta>catchword: Now</meta>

<summary>This page presents the epitaph of Paracelsus in Salzburg and the opening of his treatise on "Meteors," where he links the creation of the four elements (bodies) to the ultimate creation of Man.</summary>
<keywords>Paracelsus, Salzburg, epitaph, medicine, four elements, creation, Adam, cosmology, meteors, fire, heaven</keywords>

--- Page 6 ---
<meta>Continuity from previous page: Having introduced the four "bodies" (elements) as the materials God used to create the world for Man, Paracelsus now explains the relationship between these elements and the "fruits" they produce for human survival.</meta>

<header>The Book of / METEORS.</header>
<page-num>2</page-num>

Now know at this time that the four <term>bodies</term> <note>original Latin: "Corpora". Paracelsus uses this term to describe the physical substance of the elements.</note> alone are mothers, in such a way that they bear fruits and assist them so that Man may sustain himself. As an example: the Earth bears its fruits; Man sustains himself from those fruits and not from the Earth itself. Likewise, the Heaven is a separate body in itself, from which alone fruits go forth to maintain mankind. How the Heaven shows and gives its fruit, I will present in this book. And I have presented this chapter for the sole reason that the Heaven is a body for itself, without any other, and gives its fruit to Man, just as the Earth does.

### OF THE FATHER OF THE ELEMENTS,
### and the mother of the Elements.
### Chapter 1.
<note>original Latin title: "DE PATRE ELEMENTORUM, & matre Elementorum. Cap. 1."</note>

Now know further what has been said of the four elements: that there are four elements which Man must have, and they are divided into the four <term>temperaments</term> <note>original German: "Complexionen". In the science of Paracelsus's time, these were the "complexions"—combinations of hot, cold, moist, and dry—thought to govern all matter and health.</note>

<column-break/>

Temperaments: But it should not be as the ancients <note>Referring to classical philosophers like Aristotle or Galen, whose rigid definitions Paracelsus often challenged.</note> have written—namely, that the Earth is cold and dry. That is not so. Rather, in some places it is cold and dry, in others cold and moist, in others hot and moist, and likewise hot and dry. But it is one element—that is, it is a Mother of fruits. And that is what an element is: a Mother of the things that sustain Man, and not defined by its temperament.

So it is also with the water, of which it is said that it is cold and moist, yet it is often hot and dry, and cold and dry, etc. For the water is not created so that it must be cold and moist; rather, it should certainly be wet, and the other qualities do not matter. However, the wetness is often dry or hot in its power, but the body is wet in its physical nature. The earth is dry so that the fruit may have its progress out of it and within it.

Likewise, the Heaven is not of one temperament, but of many. It is not a fire, yet it is regarded as fire. It *is* the fire in this way: a fire comes from it; sometimes it is water, sometimes fire, sometimes cold, sometimes warm, etc. This much you should know and understand: that the elements are mothers alone, and are not to be forced into a single temperament, but rather into...

<sig>A ij</sig>

<summary>Paracelsus redefines the four elements as "mothers" whose purpose is to provide "fruit" for Man, arguing against the ancient view that elements are restricted to fixed qualities like cold or dry.</summary>
<keywords>elements, temperaments, complexions, Heaven, Earth, Water, Paracelsus, natural philosophy</keywords>

--- Page 7 ---
<meta>Continuity from previous page: Paracelsus is continuing his explanation of the four elements (Earth, Water, Air, and Heaven, which he equates to Fire) and why their physical properties or "bodies" must differ to serve their specific purposes in sustaining life.</meta>

<header>The Book of / METEORS.</header>
<page-num>3</page-num>

...but in many ways: For just as that is which grows from it, so too is that which has made and birthed it; as <term>Spearwort</term> <note>original Latin: "Flamula". Likely *Ranunculus flammula*, a plant known for its "fiery" acridity.</note> comes from Spearwort, and <term>Nightshade</term> <note>original Latin: "Solatrum".</note> from Nightshade.

Now note this: the Earth is meant to carry Man and be a dwelling, as well as for stones, rocks, sand, and all plants. From this it follows that it must be an element that is <term>compact</term> <note>original Latin: "Compactum". Dense or solid.</note> and firm, so that it may carry such things. From this it follows that it is hard, for it must be tilled with the plow. Now the water must be wet, for the reason that fish must swim in it, which cannot happen in the Earth. And it shall give salt and give stone. All such things must be wet in their <term>prime matter</term> <note>original: "erste Matery" / "Materia prima". In Paracelsian alchemy, this refers to the fundamental, unformed base of a substance.</note>, and from that wetness it arrives at a <term>coagulation</term> <note>The process of a liquid becoming a solid or semi-solid state.</note>. But whatever comes from the Earth has a seed, a body that is dry, such as seeds, roots, and trees; and all are from dry prime matter that is compact. In water, however, there is nothing in the prime matter that is a compacted body; instead, all is a <term>liquid</term> <note>original Latin: "Liquor".</note>, which has its mother from the element of water. And in the element of water grow the <term>ultimate matters</term> <note>original Latin: "Vltimæ materiæ". The final, completed form of a substance after it has matured.</note>, whose beginning is <term>fluid</term> <note>original Latin: "Liquidum".</note> and <term>moist</term> <note>original Latin: "Humidum".</note>.

Now the third body is the Air. Now

<column-break/>

that same element must also have a body, such that this same body is not moist like water, nor firm like the earth. For the reason is that all things that grow from the elements have their entrance into the <term>body of the air</term> <note>original Latin: "Corpus Aeris".</note>, just as a fish is in the water. Man is placed into the <term>aerial body</term> <note>original Latin: "Corpus Aereum".</note> to walk within it, just as the fish is in the water; so too all trees and everything that grows. Therefore, it is necessary that the Air be a <term>chaos</term> <note>original: "Chaos". Paracelsus uses this term here to describe the atmosphere as a transparent, formless void or "gap" that fills the space between things.</note>—not an earth, not a water—but <term>transparent</term> <note>original Latin: "Perspicuum".</note> and <term>clear</term> <note>original Latin: "Diaphanum".</note>, intangible and invisible, so that the tangible may be within it and that which is visible may be seen through it; and it is like a glass.

Thus know further that the Heaven likewise is also a body that is not compact, not firm like the Earth, not wet like the water, and not transparent like the Air. Rather, it has a special body with a different nature, so that the Heaven is not <term>terrestrial</term> <note>original Latin: "Terrestrisch". Earthly.</note>, and yet it is a compact being—though not an earthly compact. It is thin and permeable like water, yet it is no water; it is clear and transparent, yet not like the Air. Rather, it is a body like a smoke, which distinguishes itself in its nature and <term>corporeality</term> <note>original: "Corporalitet".</note> from all other bodies.

A iij

<vocab>Element, Body, Prime Matter, Coagulation, Chaos, Transparent, Clear, Air, Heaven, Corporeality</vocab>

<summary>Paracelsus describes the unique physical properties of the four elements, explaining how Earth is solid for walking, Water is liquid for swimming, Air is a transparent "chaos" for movement and sight, and Heaven is a smoke-like substance.</summary>
<keywords>elements, earth, water, air, heaven, alchemy, prime matter, chaos, transparency, Paracelsus</keywords>

<meta>Continuity: On the previous page, Paracelsus established the distinct physical requirements for the elements of Earth and Water. He now transitions to the more subtle "bodies" of the higher elements, particularly Heaven (Fire) and Air.</meta>

<header>The Book of / METEORS.</header>
<page-num>4</page-num>
<meta>catchword: and / together</meta>

Bodies. <note>original Latin: "Corporibus."</note> **That is / it is not like a stone or wood or earth / nor like a water / not like the air / but / is a Body / that has no mixture with the others at all: So also is the Heaven a Body / which can best be understood through smoke alone. For it must be so / for the reason that / what grows from it / is also so / they are bodies and yet not bodies / are compact and yet not compact / are penetrable like water and yet not water / are intangible / like the air / yet not the air. Likewise the Sun / which is a body as mentioned / the moon and all stars.**

Now it is without <term>complexion</term> <note>The specific balance of qualities (hot, cold, moist, dry). Paracelsus argues the fundamental element is pure and lacks these secondary qualities.</note> / and is the element of fire / and a Mother / out of which the fire grows and arises. Just as fire has a corporeality in it / so too does the Heaven / so too the stars which take their nature and essence from the Heaven. Therefore, know now regarding the Heaven / that this same body is a special part / and an element of fire / and what fire is meant to do, the Heaven does / which gives it and from which it grows.

Also know henceforth about the color of the Heaven. The Earth has a color through and through black / and whatever is another color / is a <term>mineral</term> <note>original: "Miner." In Paracelsian terms, this refers to a specific substance formed or "grown" within the matrix of an element.</note>. The water also has a color through

<column-break/>

and through, and whatever other colors exist / is also a mineral. Now the color of the water has no name / is not white / is not gray / nor blue nor green / and yet may be counted among them all / or regarded as such. The Earth also has a color / it is not black / it is not brown etc. and yet something like them. So it is also with the air / there one sees a pure color / transparent in the <term>Chaos</term> <note>original: "Chaos." For Paracelsus, this specifically refers to the atmosphere or the space occupied by the element of Air.</note> / is not white / not brown / not yellow / etc. and yet something like these colors.

In the same way the Heaven is colored / is like green / like blue / like brown / etc. and yet is none of those colors / and may be regarded as such. For the elemental bodies are such / that no perfect color can be called by name. But what comes from them / that has its clear, understandable, and named color. Since the elements themselves do not have perfect colors / but as has been mentioned: Because not just one color grows from them / but many colors / therefore they are composed of so many colors / in so many / as many as come from them: As from the Earth comes green / red / blue / black etc. So also from the water all colors: So also from the air and from the Heaven. Therefore they are <term>collected colors</term> <note>original Latin: "Colores Collecti."</note> of all kinds of colors /

->together<-

<summary>Paracelsus describes the paradoxical nature of the "Body" of Heaven (Fire), comparing its substance to smoke—neither fully solid nor fully intangible. He also explains that while the four elements themselves have no single "perfect" color, they contain the potential for all colors seen in the minerals and life forms that grow from them.</summary>
<keywords>element of fire, celestial bodies, smoke, alchemy, chaos, color theory, minerals, Paracelsian cosmology</keywords>

<meta>Continuity: This page continues Paracelsus's discussion on the physical nature of the elements. Having previously described the "Body" of the Heaven as being like smoke—intangible yet distinct—he now explains how celestial bodies (stars) exist within that medium by comparing them to life on Earth and in Water.</meta>

<header>The Book of /</header>
<meta>catchword: ment (completing the word "Element")</meta>

...mixed together into one, and the mixture gives no colors—that is, it can produce no colors with a clear, intelligible, or certain name <note>Paracelsus is arguing that the pure element of fire/heaven has no specific color of its own until it produces "minerals" or "fruits."</note>.

Now, observe the same concerning the Heaven and its fruits through this example: You see what grows from the Earth; that same thing has its palpable feet and roots in the Earth, such as trees and grass. But now, the stars are fruits of the Heaven and belong to the Heaven; yet they do not take root in the Heaven. They stand before the Heaven without any support or attachment. It is exactly the opposite of the Earth: one gives its fruit with roots, the other without; one stands upwards, the other hangs downwards; one gives its fruit toward the heights, the other downwards. 

So it is also in the water, as the fish provide an example; they stand on nothing and have no feet, but rather swim in the water. In the same way, the stars swim in the Heaven also, within the <term>body</term> <note>original Latin: "Corpus"</note> of the Heaven. This is to be understood as having the same form as the Heaven, just as it is in water. And they have their order in their swimming as God created them: high, in the distance, near, fast, slow, etc. This I leave to <term>astronomy</term> <note>original: "Astronomi"</note>; there is no need to mention it here, except to understand that the Heaven is a body that can carry a swim-

<column-break/>

<page-num>5</page-num>
<header>OF METEORS.</header>
<sig>B</sig>
<meta>catchword: shows /</meta>

-ming thing like water, and yet it is not water, but dry. And regarding the dryness that swims in it—which has not "swum" in the literal sense, for it is not in water, and yet it has swum; it has not walked or run, for there are no feet, nor anything upon which they could step. Instead, it is a wondrous work of God and an element that contains all the others and encloses them together in a <term>roundness</term> <note>original: "Rotundiert"</note> and <term>circle</term> <note>original: "Circkel"</note>.

Now, also have such an understanding regarding the stars. They have grown out of the Heaven and stand in it in a flying manner, like a bird in the air, according to the order and circle as God created them and as pleased Him. They have grown only once and thus remain forever and ever. The trees and fruits of the Earth perish and return again; the stars, however, perish only once and return nowhere else—that is, at the end of the world. Otherwise, whatever lies in other elements is consumed by rust, moths, or death; only the stars of the heavenly element remain. However, their fruits—such as rain, snow, etc.—come and go.

Now they have a color for the most part, which is fiery; just as the Earth indicates green as the most common color.

<summary>Paracelsus explains that stars "swim" in the dry element of Heaven just as fish swim in water, serving as the permanent "fruits" of the celestial realm. He contrasts the eternal nature of stars with the transience of earthly plants and weather phenomena like rain and snow.</summary>
<keywords>astronomy, element of fire, celestial bodies, permanence vs. transience, cosmology, Paracelsus, meteors</keywords>

--- Page 10 ---
<meta>Continuity: This page continues the argument that the "fruits" of the Heaven (the stars) are distinct from the substances of Earth and Water. Paracelsus is now transitioning into a redefinition of the four classical elements, arguing that "Fire" is not a true element, but "Heaven" is.</meta>

<page-num>5</page-num>
<header>The Book.</header>
<meta>catchword: also (translated as: also)</meta>

...shows, and yet other colors besides; just as then the Sun, which is also thus distinct in its colors. Now although the colors are fiery, and fire is the true element, they are nevertheless not the wood-fire, but the Element: For there is a distinction between fire and the element of Heaven, such as <term>Fire</term> and <term>Heaven</term> <note>original Latin: "Ignis" and "Cœlum"</note>. Fire is not an element, but Heaven is an element. Fire is a <term>matter</term> <note>original Latin: "Materia"</note> that cooks and prepares and brings things into the <term>ultimate matter</term> <note>original Latin: "Ultimam Materiam"; in Paracelsian alchemy, the final state of a substance after transformation</note>; it is like Death. Fire and Death are alike: fire consumes everything and takes it away; so also does Death. Therefore, fire cannot be an element, but Death certainly can—a visible death, a perceptible death—whereas the other death is invisible, which no one sees nor has seen. And as Fire takes away and utterly consumes, so also Death takes away man and all that has life. Thus Death and Fire shall stand equal in one degree: And the fire that is an element is the Heaven with its fruits, which are the stars.

For concerning fire you should know that it has been reckoned as an element, to be one of the four, which cannot be: For Earth shows itself as an element, Water also—

<column-break/>

<page-num>6</page-num>
<header>OF METEORS.</header>
<sig>B ij</sig>
<meta>catchword: before</meta>

—also, the Air also. Now see where the fourth element might be: namely, it cannot be Fire, for it gives nothing elemental to man, provides no fruits, has no fellowship with man, nor man with it; except in so far as it pertains to the nature of Death, which separates soul and body from one another. Thus, the fourth element must be the Heaven: For the Heaven has fellowship with man, and man cannot exist without it; he must have it. Without fire he can certainly exist and live without fire <note>Paracelsus likely means the "artificial" fire of the hearth, as opposed to the "vital heat" or celestial influence</note>. Therefore, since he can exist without fire, fire is no element, but rather the Heaven, which gives day and night, summer and winter, and increases all fruits, and assists the other elements. Therefore it is the Element, and should not be called the <term>element of fire</term> <note>original Latin: "Elementum Ignis"</note>, but the <term>element of heaven</term> <note>original Latin: "Elementum Cœli"</note>. Just like the <term>element of earth</term>, the <term>element of air</term>, and the <term>element of water</term> <note>original Latin: "Elementum Terræ, Elementum Aeris, Elementum Aquæ"</note>.

Thus the Heaven is the fourth element, and the first element. For the <term>Scripture</term> <note>referring to Genesis 1:1</note> says that God created Heaven and Earth first; that is, the Heaven first: For this reason, that in the Heaven the other elements are enclosed within the outer element of Heaven. When before—

<summary>Paracelsus argues that Fire is not one of the four elements because it is a destructive force akin to Death; instead, he identifies "Heaven" (Cœlum) as the true fourth element because it sustains life and contains all other elements.</summary>
<keywords>elements, Fire vs. Heaven, Ignis, Cœlum, alchemy, cosmology, nature of death, creation, Genesis</keywords>

--- Page 11 ---
<meta>This page continues Paracelsus's cosmological argument. He uses the analogy of a wine barrel to explain that the "Element of Heaven" acts as a container or "receptacle" that must exist before the things it contains. He then introduces the concept of the "First Matter" (Prima Materia) and the "Fiat" (the divine word of creation), explaining how one unified command resulted in the diversity of the four elements and their various forms.</meta>

<header>The Book</header>

...before the wine exists, the barrel must exist first. Therefore, you should know that the <term>receptacle</term> <note>original German: "Reservauel"; in Paracelsian thought, a vessel or matrix that contains and sustains a substance</note> must always exist before that which is meant to be held within it. Thus, the Element of Heaven is the first element, followed by the Earth; just as first comes the barrel, and then the wine.

### ON THE FIRST MATTER
#### Of the Heaven & Stars.
#### Chapter II.

So that you may understand what kind of body the <term>Body of Heaven</term> <note>original Latin: "Corpus Cœli"</note> is: For Earth has a distinct body, as does Water, and likewise the Air; in the same way, the Heaven has a distinct body. Now you should know that all four bodies of the four elements are made out of nothing; they were created solely through the word of God, which was called <term>Fiat</term> <note>Latin: "Let it be done"; the command used by God to create the world in Genesis</note>. Although this is so, the "Nothing" from which "Something" came has become a substance and a body, as they now appear.

That same body of all four elements is divided into three species, so that the word "Fiat" became a threefold body; that is, divided
->into<-

<column-break/>

<header>OF METEORS. 7</header>
<page-num>7</page-num>

into three kinds of bodies: For the Earth is three parts—that is, of three kinds—and Water is also threefold in its body, as is the Air and the Heaven. Just as if one were to take gold, silver, and copper and make four different instruments from them, so that no one was like the other; yet each would be like the other in its substance, though not in its form.

Although it also divides itself within the elements, it is not as if all four were one body: For the Air is one body, the Water another, the Earth another, and so the Heaven as well. Even if four drinking vessels have the same substance, the example is meant only to help you understand that the Earth has three types of bodies, as do the Air, the Heaven, and the Water.

And as you see that wood is one substance, metal another, stone another, and fungus another: so also are the four bodies of the elements distinctly divided from one another. For example, if one takes <term>lead</term> <note>original German: "Pley"</note> and makes from it <term>minium</term> <note>red lead pigment</note>, and <term>ceruse</term> <note>white lead pigment</note>, and glass, and a <term>spirit of Saturn</term> <note>original Latin: "Spiritum Saturni"; in alchemy, this refers to lead acetate or a distilled essence of lead</note>: Now look, all four are lead, and yet not one of the things made from it is like lead, but each is different. Thus I wish only to have
->indicated<- here

<sig>B iij</sig>

<summary>Paracelsus explains that the "Heavenly Element" serves as a container for the stars, much like a barrel for wine, and argues that all elements were created from "Nothing" by the divine command ("Fiat") into three-fold distinct substances.</summary>
<keywords>First Matter, Element of Heaven, creation, Fiat, alchemy, lead, minium, spirit of Saturn, four elements</keywords>

--- Page 12 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 7 regarding the threefold nature of the divine "Word" (Fiat) and how it manifests in the four elements. It develops the argument that everything in creation is fundamentally composed of three parts, reflecting the Trinity, and clarifies that individual objects do not contain all four elements but are rooted in one specific "mother" element.</meta>

<header>The Book / OF METEORS.</header>
<page-num>8</page-num>

...indicated that three <term>species</term> <note>original German: "Species"; in Paracelsian thought, these refer to the three fundamental principles of Salt, Sulfur, and Mercury</note> have come from the Word, and these same three species are divided into four elements, each into a particular and different <term>body</term> <note>original Latin: "Corpus"</note>, according to how it belonged to those same elements to be, according to its <term>office</term> <note>original Latin: "Officio"; meaning its specific function or duty in the natural order</note>.

Now, no one should wonder at this philosophy—that there are three kinds, yet they are divided into four elements. For if we rightly consider the number, God took three for Himself, and out of three He made all things, and placed all things in three. For the origin of this number is from God first; that is, the beginning is three in the Godhead. Now the Word was also threefold, for the <term>Trinity</term> <note>original German: "Trinitet"</note> spoke it, and the Word is the beginning of Heaven and Earth and all <term>creatures</term> <note>original German: "Creaturen"</note>. Thus by this number we are reminded of the Trinity in the three species, and henceforth all things are set in three; and there is nothing on Earth that does not have its being in the three species, and it is brought back again into three bodies; so that it is visible and proves itself, that every creature may be divided into those three pieces, each to its own place.

What is said about the "four things"—that is, the four elements—suggesting that every single thing contains all four elements within it: that is not the case. Rather, a thing has within it one <term>complexion</term> <note>original German: "Complexion"; the specific temperament or combination of qualities like heat or moisture</note> and no more, and it can have no other element in it except as much as it has from its "mother." For example, every herb has only one element, which is <term>Earth</term> <note>original Latin: "Terræ"</note>. Every stone has only one element, which is <term>Water</term> <note>original Latin: "Aqua"</note>. Furthermore, it has within it a complexion—cold or moist, cold or dry, warm or moist, warm or dry—yet that is not the element itself; rather, the "Mother" is the element, the Water or the Earth. 

As an example, Man is from the <term>slime of the Earth</term> <note>original Latin: "Limo Terræ"; a reference to the biblical creation of Adam</note>. Now, the element is not the slime itself, but the slime belongs to the element; it is the <term>fifth being</term> <note>original Latin: "Quintum esse" or Quintessence; the pure, spiritual essence extracted from the four elements</note>. But man returns again to the element; that is, he ends up in the element, but with such a distinction as is shown between the element and the flesh.

Thus you should understand in this paragraph that the elements are divided into only three parts, and these three parts are the <term>first matter</term> <note>original Latin: "Materia prima"</note> of the elements. But the first matter of <term>Water</term> <note>original Latin: "Aquæ"</note> is one thing, that of <term>Earth</term> <note>original Latin: "Terræ"</note> another, of <term>Air</term> <note>original Latin: "Aeris"</note> another, and of <term>Heaven</term> <note>original Latin: "Cœli"</note> another. For the number three alone provides the species at the foundation, which three make a perfect body, as is found in all bodies through the Art <note>Alchemy</note> within Nature.

<summary>Paracelsus explains that all created things are composed of three fundamental principles (species) reflecting the Holy Trinity. He rejects the traditional idea that every object contains all four elements, arguing instead that each thing belongs to one "mother" element.</summary>

# ON METEORS <note>original Latin: "METEORORUM"</note>

For just as the Earth is useful to Man, so too is the Heaven just as useful to him. Every element is created for the sake of Man; therefore, he ought to know it well.

<margin>Note Well 3</margin>
Furthermore, it is also necessary that we know about the elements—what an element is, and what it is not. So understand first this example: A human being has a large body and many kinds of beings within that body; but that which makes the human being is a soul, is a spirit, and is small. The reason the <term>body</term> <note>original Latin: "Corpus"</note> is called a "human" is because the human is *within* the body, and the soul does not exist without the body, but rather they are together. The eye is large on a human, but the power that actually sees is a small quantity compared to the eye itself. 

Thus, the Earth is called an element, though it is merely a bare <term>body</term>; however, the <term>element of Earth</term> <note>original Latin: "Elementum Terrae"</note> is within that very body and is invisible, just as the spirit of Man is invisible. In the same way, the Heaven that we see is a great <term>body</term>; the element within it is a small spirit compared to the great size that the Heaven possesses. A human speaks from their mouth—it is not the body that speaks, the spirit speaks. Likewise, the Earth bears fruit—not the physical earth itself, but the element within it, which gives the fruit.

Thus, the four elements are <term>physical</term> <note>original German: "Corporalisch"</note>, but in their essence and nature they are spirits. God is wonderful in His creations—He is not like a carpenter who builds a house out of physical matter and cares nothing for the spirit. Such a <term>physical body</term> <note>original Latin: "Corpus"</note> would be without spirit; therefore, it would yield no fruit, it would be dead, and it would not be alive anywhere. For the spirit is living, and life is the spirit; life and spirit perform all things, yet they are one thing and not two.

Therefore, know that when it is said "this comes from the element," you must understand it as coming from the element and not from the physical body. The tongue speaks, yet it speaks nothing of itself, for it is the spirit within it that speaks; the flesh does nothing on its own. But through such mixing and union, the physical body is thought to do all things, and is even considered to be the element itself, even though it is only a body and not the spirit. Just as flesh and blood are not the "human"—the spirit is the human. In this same way, there are four souls or spirits: one is the Earth, the second is the Water, the third is the Air, and the fourth is the Heaven.

Now, understand further based on this: all things and fruits grow upon the Earth, and whatever grows out of her also has the <term>Element of Earth</term> as its mother. Namely, it has a physical body, and within that body a <term>spirit</term> <note>original Latin: "Spiritum"</note>. The spirit is the true essence; the rest is nothing. The <term>boxwood tree</term> <note>original Latin: "Buxus"</note> has a taste; that very taste is its spirit, while the rest is the physical body. <term>Musk</term> <note>original German: "Bisem"</note> has its soul in its scent <note>original German: "geschmack"; here referring to the sensory essence</note>; if that is lost, it is like a piece of dead, hewn wood that yields no more fruit. Just as it is with taste—that is, the spirit is the taste—so it is with <term>coral</term>: the color is the spirit, and the rest is a physical body.

Thus, all fruits are like their element in both body and spirit. It is not that which the eyes see that is the fruit; rather, the fruit is solely that which the spirit is. However, there is a difference between a natural spirit and an unnatural one. The natural spirit is physical and naturally embodied in a specific body; the unnatural spirit is entirely without a body. For although the redness of the coral *is* the coral, and the physical body is not, nevertheless the redness is still a body—but that body belongs so entirely to the spirit that nothing foul or coarse remains. And because the spirit of fruits is neither tangible nor visible, Nature has given it a special body to reside in, and

place. But if the shell were now to break, it would fall apart, and it would not be what it is within the shell. the white and the water, such that in the white there is a <term>chaotic nature</term> <note>original: "Caosische arth"; referring to a state of suspension or the medium of the "Chaos" in Paracelsian thought</note> so that nothing can fall through it. ### ON THE FIRST
### Matter and Essence of the Stars.
### Chapter III.

As you have now heard concerning the <term>First Matter</term> <note>original Latin: "Prima materia"; the fundamental substance from which all things are created</note> of the stars and the heavens—that there are three <term>species</term> <note>likely referring to the Paracelsian "Tria Prima": Salt, Sulfur, and Mercury</note> in one body, under one name—now learn further regarding the Heaven and how it exists in its <term>physicality</term> <note>original: "Corporaliteten"</note>. First, observe this example: the Earth is black, coarse, clumsy, bony, bare, muddy, foul, slimy, and so forth; it is so coarse that nothing is coarser. Now, the element of Water is more <term>subtle</term> <note>original: "subtiler"; in this context meaning finer, thinner, or less dense</note>; it is lucid, clear, and pure, so that one can see several feet to the bottom. Thus, the Water is clarified far above the Earth. After that is the Air, which is entirely transparent, intangible, and so purely purged that one sees nothing of it at all. Now measure how unlike these <term>bodies</term> <note>original: "Corpora"</note> are to one another:

<column-break/>

the Earth compared to the Water, and the Water compared to the Air—what a vast difference there is. Now, behold the Heaven, which is above the Air. How far apart are the Heaven and the Earth from one another in their physicality! As coarse as the Earth is, the Heaven is just as subtle. It is the clearest element, and yet it is still a <term>body</term> <note>original Latin: "Corpus"</note>, for its fruits are bodies—such as rain, snow, hail, lightning bolts, and so forth. Where there is no body, nothing can be born; but where a body exists, something is born.

Now one can see the body of a lightning bolt—that it is a body just as surely as wood which comes from the Earth. But they are as far apart from each other as the elements themselves are apart. As you see, how wood compares to a lightning bolt, or to snow; or how a stone compares to dew, and so forth. And as much as the Heaven is more subtle than the Earth, so much more is its fruit superior to the fruits of other elements in its subtlety and <term>operation</term> <note>original: "Operation"; its active power or effect</note>. For trees are recognized by their fruits—from what they are made and from whence they have come. For every tree makes itself known through its own fruit. And it is with the fruit that <term>Philosophy</term> begins, and not with the mother, but rather with the child. The master is known by his work, and the work

<margin>Concerning the Stars.</margin>
Now moving further: after enough has been said regarding the Heaven, it is now most necessary to know about the stars and their origin. And it is in this way: You see that the Earth has nothing within itself, but rather whatever it possesses, it pushes out onto the Earth's [surface], and it keeps nothing inside, but gives everything forth. Thus, all trees and herbs are from the Earth and upon the Earth, and there is nothing [left] inside the Earth. In the same way, know that all the <term>constellations</term> <note>original: "Gestirn"</note> have grown out of the Heaven and were taken from it. And the stars do not stand before the Heaven any differently than trees stand upon the Earth. Yet, unlike the trees on Earth which have their "foot" in the ground, the stars have no foot in their element. The reason for this is that trees must stand fixed and not move from their spot, but remain in the place where they are set, sown, and planted. The stars, however, must not stand still, but must traverse their circle. For this reason, they are spheres that roll and wander continuously, as if they were thrown by the hand of God. And so they are distinct from the Heaven, yet still in the Heaven. Just as the tree is 

->incorporated<-

incorporated into the Earth and cannot exist without the Earth, but must be in it and have it, so too the stars do not leave the Heaven, for they have their nourishment from the Heaven. And where they have their <term>mansions</term> <note>original Latin: "Mansiones"; the specific positions or "houses" of the stars in the sky</note>, there they remain, and from those places they do not depart, any more than a tree leaves its garden.

Therefore, understand now regarding the stars: how they stand in their element, how they are incorporated into their element, and how they are fruits just like the trees on Earth. And this should also be noted: just as there are many kinds and communities of stars, there are just as many varieties of growth on the Earth. For every specific kind of tree, there is a specific kind of star. This applies not only to trees, but also to herbs and all earthly growths. This is proven by the fact that as many <term>influences</term> <note>original: "jnfluenz"; the invisible "flow" of power or liquid-like essence from the stars to the Earth</note> as there are, so many types of stars exist, and so many such growths exist upon the Earth. Each type goes toward its own kind, and unlike things do not come together. This is sufficiently found and proven in <term>Philosophy</term> and <term>Astronomy</term>. For every birth is uniform with the other, and equal in number.

Now you shall further know: just as you see that fruits come from the trees, and that...

<header>The Book / OF METEORS.</header>
<page-num>16</page-num>

just as nature in a specific place is an inherent property and an essence—that is, a gifted talent that makes a pear tree bear pears, another apples, another nuts, etc.—so too are the stars positioned in such specific roles. One star is born for rain, another for snow, another for hail, and so forth. Thus, everything that comes from the heavens is born in this specific form.

Now look at the Sun and what is within it: namely, the heat that shines upon us. This heat is innate to it; it is its inherent property to be hot. Just as we see with the <term>Etnean fires</term> <note>Refers to the volcanic fires of Mount Etna</note> or with hot springs, the stars possess such a nature, only much greater and stronger, insofar as the <term>Element</term> <note>The celestial element or "air" in this context</note> is above the Earth and all that is earthly. Therefore, you can see the coldness of the <term>Crystal Stars</term> <note>Refers to the belief that certain stars were composed of a crystalline, ice-like substance</note>. Indeed, there are stars endowed with such coldness as part of their very being. Just as you see a magnet pull things toward itself, there are also "attractive" stars in the <term>firmament</term> <note>The sphere of the fixed stars</note>. And just as there are things of a dry or moist nature on Earth, so it is in the heavens throughout the whole firmament.

Therefore, the <term>operations of the stars</term> <note>original: "Operationes Stellarum"</note> should be understood as coming from their own inherent properties, which are given to them in this way. And such operations are found within the <term>Three Firsts</term> <note>original: "drey ersten". In Paracelsian philosophy, these are Salt, Sulfur, and Mercury—the fundamental building blocks of all matter</note>; they emerge from these, as will be described in the following sections.

For this reason, the traditional study of <term>meteorology</term> <note>original: "Meteorica". The author is critiquing the Aristotelian view that weather is caused by vapors drawn up from the earth by the sun's heat</note> is false when it speaks of the sun’s heat, its movement, or how other generations arise by being drawn up from the Earth. It is not as they say; rather, it is a <term>nature that is endowed and appropriated</term> <note>original: "natura appropriata, dotata"</note>, understood in the same way as all other creatures on Earth. There is no star that "pulls" rain to itself only to give it back again later (or any such thing). Instead, it is the star's own nature, just as it is the nature of the pear tree to produce pears. Whatever the Sun dries up is consumed in the air, just as all things are consumed in the Earth. And just as the death of those things that come from the Earth happens within the Earth—so that all things come to nothing and are entirely consumed—so too is there such a "grave" and consumption of all moist things within the air.

<margin>Summer Winter.</margin>
Now, know this regarding summer and winter: they also come from the stars. For in summer, there are "Summer Stars" that provide the summer, and there are other stars that provide the winter. The Sun is the first and highest Summer [star]...

<summary>Paracelsus argues that weather phenomena like rain, snow, and heat are not caused by earthly vapors but are the "innate fruit" of specific stars. He rejects traditional Aristotelian meteorology in favor of a theory where stars have inherent properties similar to how different trees bear different fruits.</summary>

These begin at the start of summer and increase in their heat. Just like a withered tree that has nothing in winter, but towards summer develops buds, then leaves, then blossoms, then its fruit, and so on. So it is with stars of this kind: in winter, they stand like dry trees, but when the time comes for the stars to rise, they take their power from the Sun and ascend, growing ever stronger in heat until they reach their hottest point. After that, they decrease in their heat. Then the winter stars begin, just like the summer stars, and "bloom" in their own nature, but toward summer they wither away. If such stars are powerful, it results in a colder winter; if the summer stars are powerful, they give a hot summer. For just as one year is good, another even better, and another worse or not good at all, so too are these stars inconsistent in their rising and setting.

Of the winter stars, the Moon is the chief, and it has many others in its nature and company. Therefore, know that if the summer stars did not exist, there would be no summer. For the Sun remains at the same heat, whether it be high or low; if there were no summer stars, it would be winter forever. For the Moon and its stars are cold forever and ever. This cycle only breaks because both types of stars exist; therefore, they "wither away"—one part in winter, the other in summer. The height of the Sun does not produce summer, nor does its long path; the summer stars produce the summer, though they take their growth from the Sun. The Moon does not create winter by itself alone; it only creates winter alongside its stars, for it is too weak on its own. Likewise, the Sun is too weak without its helpers.

<margin>Day and Night.</margin>
In the same way, know also of day and night: namely, that the day comes from the light of the Sun, and the night from the light of the Moon. And in this way, God created these <term>day-qualities</term> <note>original: "tagheiten"; refers to the essential properties that constitute daylight</note> in the Sun, just like brightness in fire, light, or the Moon. And He made two kinds of light in the <term>firmament</term> <note>The sphere of the stars and planets</note>: a day-light and a night-light, neither being like the other. One is white, the other red. Now when the day-light rises, the day begins, and the night withdraws. For the day-light has the brilliance to blind all other lights. Now, when the day-light sets and has completed its course as commanded by God, then there goes...

<summary>This page explains that seasons are caused by specific "summer" and "winter" stars that wax and wane in power, rather than by the Sun's position alone. It also attributes the distinction between day and night to two different types of light—white and red—created by God within the celestial bodies.</summary>

<keywords>Summer stars, Winter stars, Sun, Moon, Day and Night, Firmament, seasonal cycles, celestial light</keywords>

->### THE BOOK OF METEORS.<-
<page-num>18</page-num>
<sig>E ij</sig>
<meta>catchword: these; catchword: rest</meta>

# The Book

governs the night-light—that is, the Moon and the darkness.

Regarding the night, you should have this understanding: it is not the departure of the Sun that creates the night, simply because the Sun has set. For its glow is so powerful that even if the Sun were directly beneath us at midnight, its light would still cast a reflection <term>Reflexum: a reflected light</term> upon the Earth, providing so much daylight that one could recognize the day and enjoy its light. But this is what the Night is, and for this reason the darkness of night descends: it is a specific kind of stars that create darkness and obscure so much—whenever the Moon is absent—that nothing can be seen at all.

Just as a gloomy cloud passing before the Sun also takes away the daylight, or as an eclipse <note>original: "finsternuß," here referring specifically to the darkening of the sun</note> occurs which takes the sunshine and creates a night. Likewise, there are specific shadows <term>Tenebræ: Latin for darkness or shadows</term> in the heavens; as soon as the Sun shines upwards or downwards, these shadows fall upon it and bring about a total darkness. In this darkness, only the Moon has its glow, yet it does no more than allow the darkness to create and form a night, until the Sun overcomes these 

---

these shadows again during its rising. Also, when the Sun loses its glow during an eclipse <term>Eclipsi: an astronomical eclipse</term> and shadows arise, it activates the night-stars, so that it becomes completely night because the Sun cannot shine through them.

Thus, God has given the stars their course, so that they are cast by the hand of God into the circle of the entire firmament, each star into its own orbit and path. The reason they must never rest <note>original: "feiren," literally to be idle or to keep a holiday</note> is so that in the absence of one star, the others may also have their operations <term>Operationes: the active influences or functions of celestial bodies</term>. Thus, in the absence of the Sun, the night-stars may also be recognized. This is the same for all other stars: now high, then low; when one is high, the other is low; when one is in exaltation <term>Exaltation: the position in which a planet has its greatest power</term>, the other is in declination <term>Declination: the setting or weakening of a star's influence</term>. This ensures that every star fulfills the effect for which God created and ordained it, with each yielding to the other so that all things may be accomplished, as we see by the example of the Sun.

For no star rests, no star stands still; they are all in daily exercise, thereby awakening Nature and driving it into daily activity. And just as the stars are without any rest, so too are without any rest

<summary>This page explains a pre-modern theory of night, suggesting that darkness is an active force created by "night-stars" and celestial shadows rather than a mere absence of sunlight. It also describes the perpetual motion of the stars as a divine order designed to keep the natural world in constant activity.</summary>

...liquor <note>original: "quore," the conclusion of "Liquore"</note> as a Spirit drawn up, and the Sun was created. After that, the radiance of the Red was placed into the **Body of Sulfur** <term>Corpus Sulphuris: in Paracelsian thought, Sulfur represents the principle of light, fire, and structure</term>, and the Red Translucence and Clarity <note>original: "Diaphinitet vnd Perspicuitet"</note> were laid into the body of Sulfur. Thus is a **Body of the Sun** <term>Corpus Solis</term>, Sulfur is a **Body of the Moon** <term>Corpus Lunæ</term>, and the Liquor is a Body of the darkness.

In this way we should also know that God created all things to be in the first **Prime Matter** <term>Prima materia: the raw, undifferentiated "stuff" of the universe before it was given specific form</term>. But He Himself separated them out again, each into that which it was meant to be. Therefore, God worked for six days until He extracted, separated, and formed the **Ultimate Matters** <term>Vltimas materias: the final, perfected state of a substance</term> from the Prime Matter. That is, every creature that He commanded to remain until the dissolution of the world.

Thus God is the same one who first created and placed all things into one, and thereafter in six days separated out what was within that same thing. For we must note this example—we can go no further with natural understanding: it is just like a **Potter** <note>original: "Haffener"</note>, who has his clay before him; containing within that clay are many kinds of instruments and **Vessels** <term>Vasa</term>, and he can thus make a thousand kinds of instruments or more out of a single piece of clay. Or like a **Carver** <note>original: "Schnizler"</note>, who has a piece of wood and can make from it whatever he wishes, provided only that he knows how to separate from it that which does not belong to the work.

Thus God took, extracted, and separated all creatures from one **Mass and Matter**, so that no splinter <note>original: "spen," meaning a wood chip or fragment</note> was left over, but each one came into its **Ultimate Matter**. For this reason, He undertook to work for six days. Thus He pulled out: what belongs to the stars He made into stars; what belongs to the light He made into His light, and raised it up from the darkness; and each specifically into its kind and place, so that nowhere is there anything more to be created, but everything is sufficiently created and the number fulfilled in all creatures, species, and beings. And it is to be understood just as this example shows: the Earth is black, brown, and foul—nothing beautiful or pretty—yet nevertheless within it are green, blue, white, red, etc., all colors. There is nothing that does not come to be within it. So when the spring comes and the summer, all the colors come forth; if the Earth did not testify of itself, no one would believe it possible for them to be within it.

Thus, just as from such black soil such noble, subtle colors emerge, so also have many kinds of creatures proceeded from the first...

It uses the metaphor of colorful flowers emerging from dark soil to illustrate how the diversity of nature was hidden within the initial void.</summary>

### CONCERNING THE LIVING SPECIES
->Within the Stars.<-
### Chapter 4.

As I have spoken until now concerning the insensible creatures <note>objects without senses, such as minerals or elements</note> of the Meteoric region and its enclosure: so it is now further to be understood that there are also sensible—that is, living, moving, and intelligent—creatures in these same **Meteoric Regions** <term>Meteorischen Region: the atmosphere and the celestial spheres where weather and stars operate</term>. We should know that God created living creatures in all the elements and left nothing to be empty. Not only did He create irrational creatures, but also rational ones. For example: in the water you see the fish; in the earth, the **Mole** <note>original: "Talpam," from the Latin talpa</note>. Just as you see them there, do not think otherwise of the air and the heavens; there are similar animals that live there. In the air are the **Matene** <term>Matene: in Paracelsian thought, these are the birds or invisible spirits of the air</term> and flies; in the heavens are the **Torteleos** <term>Torteleos: specific celestial beings or "creatures of the stars" described by Paracelsus</term>. Furthermore, there are living, sensible creatures in the manner of spirits: such as the **Nymphs** in the water, the **Gnomes** in the earth, the **Lemures** in the air, and the **Penates** in the heavens.

What is shown to us by our coarse understanding in the lower **Sphere and Globe** <note>the Earthly realm</note> serves as an example that in the upper sphere and the upper globe there must also be a similar likeness. This is found to be true in the finished work, in Nature, and in the Art <note>referring to Alchemy or the "Spagyric Art"</note>.

Now, we shall not speak here of Nymphs, nor of Gnomes and Lemures; those are to be **philosophized** <note>discussed or studied scientifically</note> in their own proper places. However, I say this so that we may know in the **Meteoric Arts** <note>original: "Meteorica"</note>, through human reason and sensibility, how these beings work and exist in such elements. For consider the many wonders that happen through the upper generations <note>celestial influences</note> down into the earth, which could not happen without such an understanding. For how could it be that a ray...

<summary>Paracelsus explains that just as precious metals and gems are formed from primordial "silt," every element (earth, water, air, and heavens) is populated by specific living and spiritual beings. He introduces the idea that the "upper" celestial realms mirror the "lower" earthly realm in their population and activity.</summary>

For no one should wonder at this; it is true of Gnomes <note>elemental spirits of the Earth</note> and of Nymphs <note>elemental spirits of the Water</note>. And although I do not describe their skills here, their power will be shown in special **Chapters on Meteors** <note>original: "Capitulis Meteoricis"</note>, and even more so in **Philosophy**, in which the student of meteors may find a very good account and sufficient understanding and knowledge. But so that I may be more easily understood in those same Meteoric Chapters regarding which Higher Ones <note>original: "Superis"</note> I am speaking of, I point them out here: they are in the Firmament and dwell within it with all wisdom, understanding, and arts, and similar effects upon **Nature** here in our own sphere <note>original: "Globul"</note>, just like the little mountain-men or spirits <term>Lemuribus: spirits of the earth or 'mountain mannikins' who possess their own wisdom</term>.

Thus we should know that we humans on Earth do not possess such understanding alone—as if there were nothing else that had intellect besides man—but there are yet more in whom such understanding (and even greater) exists than in humans, in all things concerning **Nature** and natural understanding. For God is wonderful in His works and creations; He, being infinitely wondrous, commanded man (as the noblest of **Creatures**) to philosophize upon all things and to explore **Nature**, so that they might bring forth the wondrous works of God. For what do we have on Earth but to walk among divine works and to recognize them? And we should not walk in things that are not the works of God.

For the works of God are divided in two ways: into the works of **Nature**, which **Philosophy** <note>original: "Philosophia"</note> encompasses; and into the works of Christ, which...

we must organize all subjects for it and make them ourselves. Thus, in all things, man is burdened with labor / as the Prophet says / *you must eat the labor of your hands* <note>original: "die arbeit deiner hände muſtu eſſen"; a reference to Psalm 128:2.</note> / also / *in the sweat of your face shall you eat your bread* / etc. <note>original: "im ſchweiß deines angeſichts ſoltu dein brodt eſſen"; a reference to Genesis 3:19.</note> This is now imposed upon man / and that is what he does.

Furthermore, know / that there is another / who makes the things / which man otherwise makes and uses. For there must be one who makes leaves and grass in God’s stead / just as a man / must make a house for himself / God does not make it for him: So also in the Earth there is one / who makes that which comes out of it. There is also such a one in the Water / who brings together such compositions: And likewise in the Air: And thus in the Firmament also. It is not enough / for us to say / God has created it so that it returns every year: It is true / but He has set someone over it / who shall make it / form it / embody it / and ordain it / and thereafter let that same work grow through Nature. For just as little as God makes a coat for a man / but instead has set the tailor to it / just as little will the grass grow and be brought into its form without a worker.

The one now / who thus ordains things from their seeds into the **Ultimate Matter** <term>Vltimam materiam: The final, perfected state of a substance after it has been fully processed by nature or art.</term> / is the spirit **Vulcan** <note>original: "Vulcanus." In alchemy, Vulcan is the "inner alchemist" or force of fire that matures and perfects matter.</note>. And even though such is a new **Philosophy** / it does not matter: For **Aristotelian Philosophy** is heavily filled with lies and errors / and composed with many masterfully deceptive tricks / which can never be found in the light of nature. Therefore one is not to believe a man on his words alone / for the Spirit blows where it will / that is / not only in one / but also in a second and a third.

Thereupon know now of the **Vulcan** of the **Firmament** <note>The heavens or the realm of the stars and weather.</note> / who has in his power / the **Three Primes** <term>3. Prima: The "Tria Prima"—Sulfur, Mercury, and Salt—which Paracelsus believed were the fundamental components of all matter.</term> in the single **Primary Matter** <term>Prima materia: The raw, formless base material of the universe.</term> / out of which he now makes in the Firmament where he has his dwelling / everything that God commanded him to make / who composes, dispenses, and ordains / the rains / the snows / the frost / the hail / the lightning-bolt / and all that are heavenly operations / in those things he is the woodcarver / the smith, and the preparer.

This **Vulcan** is no spirit / nor is he a person: In this he differs from the **Sagani** <term>Sagani: Elemental spirits (like Gnomes or Nymphs) that inhabit the four elements.</term>. Rather / it is a worker / who does nothing more than attend to Nature / and to bring out of her that which is in her / and to bring that which God has placed in her into the **Ultimate Matter**: He is exactly a craftsman like fire / which also works a...

<summary>Paracelsus introduces Vulcan as the "inner worker" of nature, a force responsible for shaping raw matter into its final forms, such as weather and plants. He argues that God employs these natural craftsmen just as humans employ tailors, while also critiquing Aristotelian philosophy for failing to recognize these natural processes.</summary>

It is no spirit, no soul, no person, and yet it has within itself such a power to seize, to take hold of, and to prepare wonderful things. This is the **Vulcan** <note>original: "Vulcanus." In this context, Vulcan refers to the transformative power of fire or the "inner alchemist" that changes the state of matter.</note> who is subject to man. However, the **Vulcan of the Elements** cannot be made to be obedient to man, but instead has its office alone in Nature.

But since man must finish his work entirely into the **Ultimate Matter** <term>Vltima materia: The final, perfected state of a substance after it has been fully processed by nature or art.</term>, and the true **Ultimate Matters** must be accomplished through man: And the **Elemental Vulcan** makes its **Ultimate Matter** into nothing else than that which is **Intermediate Matter** <term>Media materia: Raw or semi-processed material provided by nature that requires further refinement by human craft.</term> for man. From this **Intermediate Matter**, man thereafter makes the **Ultimate Matter**. For this, he must have a Vulcan, which is the fire; this fire represents Vulcan and is the **Vulcan of Fire** <note>original: "Vulcanus Ignis."</note>. It is a servant of the natural arts that man knows, standing in place of the natural, elemental Vulcan.

Thus, as the example of the **Vulcan of Fire** demonstrates, so also is the **Elemental Vulcan** <note>original: "Vulcanus Elementatus."</note> understood, which finishes all things without the help of others. Just as the fire melts the metal—man cannot do it himself, the fire does it alone—and burns

wood—man cannot burn it himself. Such a being must exist in Nature which has a Vulcan-like quality; otherwise, nothing could come of Nature, but everything would thus die out and not reach its end.

Now observe further what else may be in Nature. Although I have here taken only **Meteorology** <note>original: "Meteoricam."</note> before me, it is necessary to introduce other philosophy here for the sake of examples, as follows: There is yet another type in the **Firmament** <note>The heavens or the sphere of the stars.</note> that shows itself in its properties and also works just like the **Fiery Vulcan**, namely this: You see that the rain from heaven makes the leaves and grass grow, without which such things could not be completed. Now, in this same rain, there is a power and nature that immediately creates and drives forth the grass and everything that grows from the earth. Thus, what the **Vulcan of the Earth** <note>original: "Vulcanus Terræ."</note> smiths, has its <margin>Iliaster.</margin> <term>Iliaster: The vital force or principle of growth and duration in nature.</term> thriving through the rain, which proceeds and increases visibly. Such an increasing, augmenting, and growing type is another nature—not the **Fiery Vulcan**, but rather it is **Iliaster**. And it is also no created spirit, nor person, nor soul, but a power—that is, a **Virtue** <note>original: "Virtus"; used here to mean an inherent power or medicinal property.</note>—which acts in this way. Likewise, <margin>Archeus.</margin> <term>Archeus: The "internal master-craftsman" or force that separates and directs substances within a body or element.</term> is another type in Nature that separates and extracts.

...draws forth the flowers or herbs from another plant, or the material of iron from the material of lead. Such a power is the **Archeus** <term>Archeus: In the philosophy of Paracelsus, this is the "inner master-builder" or vital force that separates and directs the growth of living things, acting like an internal alchemist.</term>, who organizes all things into their own being, separates one thing from another, and gives to each seed what belongs to it; that is, he gives to the **Vulcan of the Earth** <note>original: "Vulcano terrae." Referring to the creative, transformative fire within the earth.</note> his instruments and the tools for his necessity.

Know also that there is an **Iliaster** <term>Iliaster: A term used to describe the primordial force or substance of the stars and the firmament that governs the "meteors" (atmospheric events).</term> in the firmament, as well as an **Archeus**, together with the **Firmamental Vulcan**. So that the things which the heavens bring forth happen perfectly, the Vulcan is there, the Iliaster is there, and likewise the Archeus; these then pursue the entire labor until it reaches its destination. Thus the rain, snow, thunder, etc., are all forged. And when it is finished at its place, they send it down to us. For they are workers day and night for our sake and care for us by the command of God. So that we might also be prudent in our divine conduct and not be hindered, these things all come to us without our care, toil, or labor. Not even a single flower would come to us if God did not give it, just as little as a sparrow <note>original: "Sperck." Paracelsus is referencing the Gospel (Matthew 10:29) where Jesus speaks of God's care for even the smallest sparrow.</note>, as Christ says in the Gospel.

And in addition to all this, after all things are made and handed over into the hand of man, there is yet another following power that remains within the thing itself, even if the **Archeus**, **Iliaster**, and **Vulcan** are withdrawn and depart from it. This is the **Essence** <note>original: "Essentia." The fundamental, intrinsic nature or medicinal core of a substance that remains after the formative forces have done their work.</note>; these are the wonder-works that lie hidden in nature. Though these are many and varied, **Essence** is its name. This same essence is also found in the highest fruits and the **Ultimate Matters** <term>Vltimis materijs: The final, perfected state of a substance after it has been fully processed by nature or art.</term>.

Now, however, it is no less true that not everything serves man without a medium, but rather through a medium. For rain does not serve man without a medium, but through a medium—that is, leaves and grass, etc. Now, understand the same regarding the other **Meteoric Impressions** <note>original: "Meteorischen impressionibus." Phenomena such as lightning or wind that originate in the atmosphere.</note>. But there is another kind in the heavens; these serve man directly and are not fruits that are tangible, visible, or material; instead, they enter into man just as the sun does with its light.

Although the sun proves its operation more noticeably, perceptibly, and obviously than all the stars, the other stars are of such a kind as well. They prove themselves like the sun, through their **rays** <note>original: "Radios."</note> into man, yet unnoticeably, imperceptibly, and untraceably. But they are powerful, as they then prove themselves, which is also sufficiently reported in **Astronomy**.

...take on power, such as the heat in summer, the cold in winter, and likewise plagues, fevers, and other things.

Therefore, these are **Inclinations** <term>Inclinationes: In Paracelsian thought, these are astrological tendencies or "leanings" that influence human behavior or health without overriding free will.</term> that take hold; but because they do not leave a physical mark, they are not **Impressions** <term>Impressiones: Direct physical imprints or "stamps" made by celestial bodies onto the material world.</term>; they remain at the disposal of man to do as he will. After this come the **Generations**, which are the meteoric fruits of the stars. And although everything is "Meteoric" and everything is "Astronomic," there is still a distinction: the invisible things are assigned to the study of Astronomy in their part, and the visible things to their own part.

So now you know what is in the stars and what is not. Let each person recognize that what he does not find in this study of Meteors, he will find in Astronomy. Thus, all things come into and remain in their perfect order. What more there is to know about the meteoric part follows hereafter in the subsequent paragraphs and chapters.

### ON THE GENERATION OF THE WINDS. Chapter V.
<note>original Latin: "DE GENERATIONE VENTORUM. Cap. V."</note>

Since you have heard enough about the beginnings of the study of Meteors, it is now appropriate to write further about the meteoric *Generations* and *Generators* <note>original: "Generatis vnd Generantibus." Referring to things created and the forces that create them.</note>. First, we shall write of the origin and birth of the winds, and of the predestined circles of the winds. Regarding this, know first: the circle and the wind-circuit have four parts: one toward the Sunrise [East], one toward the Sunset [West], one toward Midday [South], and one toward Midnight [North].

Now the circle is positioned in such a manner: just as the two elements—Earth and Water—lie in the center of the **Firmament**, and between the element of the Heavens and the lower **Globe** lies the element of Air. Just as the Earth lies in the center and the Heaven revolves around it, so the circle goes horizontally across the surface in the middle of the Heaven and the Earth and Water, encircling them. As an example: take an egg and place it on the ground—not upright, but horizontally—and take the center point all around the egg, so that the yolk also falls into that same middle, just as the distribution naturally occurs. Now note this: just as the Heaven and the world move together within a circle, so in that same circle are the "mothers" and places of the winds, from which the winds arise as they are predestined. And when they wish to go forth, they travel from that same circle toward the globe through the element of air. And when they reach the **Rotundity** <term>Rotunditet: The curvature or spherical shape of the earth and its surrounding atmospheric layers.</term> of the world-spheres and strike...

## 29
<meta>catchword: to the / other</meta>
H

strike against it; so it may well be that the wind either beats under the spheres <note>original: "kugeln." Paracelsus refers to the celestial or elemental spheres that make up the cosmos.</note>—those that are beneath us—or upon the spheres of those who dwell above, or both ways, as it divides itself according to the height or the depth of the spheres. And so the wind travels through the air over the sea and earth, as far and as long as it exists, until through long exercise and wide travel it consumes itself, and is driven away and vanishes, so that no **matter** <term>Materia: The physical substance or "stuff" from which a natural phenomenon is made.</term> remains there anymore.

<margin>The East Wind.</margin>
Now it is no less true that there are four parts, and each part has a special character within itself. Namely, the part from the **East** <note>original: "Orient."</note> is warm and dry. Not that it has this from the Sun—that it should be warm and dry because of it—or because it stands in the East; no, but rather because its nature is thus in the **Three Firsts** <term>Three Firsts: Also known as the *Tria Prima* (Sulfur, Salt, and Mercury), these are the three fundamental spiritual substances that Paracelsus believed composed all material things.</term>, to be so, and not against its nature. Therefore, in the true **Eurus** <note>The Latin name for the East wind.</note> and its companions, no other nature or operation is found; all are warm and dry.

<margin>The West Wind.</margin>
Conversely, the **Zephyrus** <note>The Latin name for the West wind.</note> in the setting of the Sun is cold and moist. This is not by reason of the sunset, nor because it stands there—for the setting has the same **complexion** <term>Complexion: The combination of qualities (hot, cold, moist, dry) that determine the temperament or nature of a thing.</term> as the rising—but the nature of the wind's matter in the West is created to be cold and moist.

Again, from the **North** <note>original: "Mitternacht," literally "Midnight," a common term for the North.</note>, those same wind-givers are of a cold and dry nature. Thus, they also make cold and dry regions wherever they are in those same parts. It is not that the winds take their nature from the regions, but rather the regions take theirs from the winds, as they are of that same nature. In the same way, in the **South** <note>original: "Mittag," literally "Midday."</note>, the **Auster** <note>The Latin name for the South wind.</note> is warm and moist, contrary to the **Septentrio** <note>The Latin name for the North wind.</note>. This is not because it has much sea, or stands in moist places, but because its nature is such, and thus it gives its **generations**.

Therefore, understand that the winds have no nature from the outside, but from within themselves. If the **Auster** were of a dry nature like the **Boreas** <note>Another Latin name for the North wind.</note>, no moisture would befall it that could make it moist; rather, it would remain dry, regardless of the sea, etc. Likewise, if a moist character were given to the **Boreas**, it would also be moist and not dry, no matter what the region was like.

Thus you should now know that the birth of the winds lies in the **Wind-Circle** <note>The horizon or the celestial wheel from which winds are thought to originate.</note> and in their own nature. They are in the **stars**, and the stars are their mothers. Of these, there are many on the Boreal side, the Eural, the Zephyric, and the Austral; not one alone, nor three, but—

<summary>Paracelsus explains that the four primary winds (East, West, North, and South) possess inherent qualities—such as heat, cold, dryness, or moisture—which are dictated by their internal nature and the stars that birthed them, rather than by the geography they pass over.</summary>

...others, many thousands, of which there is no number. These are the ones from which all winds arise, which are set in the main circle. Although winds also arise from other stars of rain or showers, they are not counted in the Wind-Circle <note>original: "Windzirckel." Paracelsus envisions a specific celestial belt or ring where the primary winds are generated.</note>, but only the four parts.

In the same way, as you may understand by a tree, which gives its fruit from itself, out of its own nature, out of its wood, and out of its marrow: So also is it to be understood with such stars.

Now the seed from which the winds become is the **Prime Matter** <term>Prima materia: The fundamental "first matter" or raw material of the universe.</term> of the **Three Firsts** <term>Three Firsts: Also known as the Tria Prima (Sulfur, Salt, and Mercury), the three spiritual substances Paracelsus believed made up all physical matter.</term>, namely of **Sulfur, Salt, [and] Liquor** <note>original: "Sulphur, Sal, Liquor." In this context, Paracelsus uses "Liquor" to refer to the third principle, usually called Mercury or Quicksilver.</note>. These three are the mothers from which grow those which we call winds: the Sulfur, Salt, and Liquor. And in the northern parts <note>original: "Mitternacht theilen," literally "midnight parts," a common archaic German term for the North.</note> they are of a cold and dry nature; those toward the south <note>original: "Mittag," literally "midday," meaning the South.</note> are warm and moist; likewise warm and dry are those in **Eurus** <note>The East wind.</note>, and cold and moist those in **Zephyrus** <note>The West wind.</note>. As the nature of the Three Firsts is, so is their fruit and produce; that is, such are those same winds.

<column-break/>

Thereupon, know that they have their growth in those same stars, a common progression according to their time: although sometimes less, sometimes more, they nevertheless rule in their times. When the winds become ripe, they receive in that time of becoming their property and nature, just as the Three Firsts are; these they retain. But because they have such a long way to travel, they become somewhat weak in their powers at certain times: as for instance, **Boreas** <note>The North wind.</note> in summer cannot have its full property, for the summer stars warm it; but in winter it can, for then it falls among the winter stars and remains unbroken. However, when winter wishes to depart, it still has its power until it is tempered by the summer stars. Now, it [the North wind] is sulfurous, therefore it is prone to cause sulfurous diseases; it is saline, therefore it splits open the mouth and its property becomes just like salt-drops from salted meat; for this reason, it causes much more **Scabies** <term>Scabies: Here referring generally to itchy, crusty skin eruptions or "the itch."</term> and **Alopetias** <term>Alopetias: A condition of hair loss or mange, which Paracelsus attributes here to the corrosive salt-nature of the wind.</term>, etc. The southern **Auster** <note>The South wind.</note> is also the same in its time, at its best in its radiance. Thus each has its time in the year in which it is specifically born and operates without its own disruption.

<summary>Paracelsus explains that winds are birthed by specific stars and are composed of the "Three Firsts" (Sulfur, Salt, and Liquor), which determine their temperature, moisture, and medicinal effects. He describes how the North wind's salty nature causes skin diseases and how seasonal stars temper the strength of these winds.</summary>
